### MACROBENTHIC INVERTEBRATE COMMUNITIES

by Barry D. Smith

#### INTRODUCTION

--- SENTENCE 1034 ---
The baseline survey of marine nonscleractinian macrobenthos of the Fagatele Bay National Marine Sanctuary was performed in April 1985 (Birkeland et al., 1987), soon after the sanctuary was officially established.

--- SENTENCE 1035 ---
Echinoderms were the predominant benthic invertebrates in terms of standing crop, but gastropods constituted the most diverse assemblage.

--- SENTENCE 1036 ---
A rapid ecological assessment of Fagatele Bay was performed during the American Samoa Coastal Resources Inventory in 1992.

--- SENTENCE 1037 ---
Although macroinvertebrates were surveyed in this program, the qualitative nature of the rapid assessment technique precluded all but broad comparisons with earlier baseline study.

--- SENTENCE 1038 ---
The purpose of this study was to reassess the macrobenthos assemblage of the Fagatele Bay National Marine Sanctuary 10 years after the original baseline study.

--- SENTENCE 1039 ---
For comparison, macroinvertebrates at 10 additional sites around Tutuila, American Samoa were surveyed by the same methods used during the baseline study.

#### METHODS

Conspicuous, epibenthic macroinvertebrates other than scleractinian corals were censused by either of two methods.

--- SENTENCE 1040 ---
The belt transect method ("line transect" in Birkeland *et al.,* 1987) was used throughout the survey, except for one species.

--- SENTENCE 1041 ---
Transects were established in the same zones as in the baseline survey in 1985.

--- SENTENCE 1042 ---
On the forereef slope, a 30-m transect line was placed along the 10-, 15-, 30-, and 40-ft isobaths and approximately parallel to shore.

--- SENTENCE 1043 ---
Macroinvertebrates occurring within 1 m on both sides of the transect line were identified and recorded along 5-m intervals of the line.

--- SENTENCE 1044 ---
Therefore, each transect on the forereef consisted of 6 quadrats, each covering an area of 10 m2.

--- SENTENCE 1045 ---
Transects on the reef flat were placed perpendicular to shore, extending from the waterline to the reef margin.

--- SENTENCE 1046 ---
Therefore, the number of 10 m2 quadrats sampled on the reef flat was a function of the extent of reef flat development.

--- SENTENCE 1047 ---
Quantification of asexually reproducing, colonial organisms, such as alcyonacean corals and encrusting sponges, poses some difficulties for the belt transect method because determination of what constitutes an individual is problematic.

--- SENTENCE 1048 ---
For the purposes of this study, alcyonacean corals and encrusting sponges were counted as individuals only when they were not connected by any tissue to surrounding clones.

--- SENTENCE 1049 ---
Interconnected clones were counted as single individuals.

--- SENTENCE 1050 ---
The small, boring echinoid *Echinostrephus aciculatus* was too numerous to count by the belt transect method on oblique limestone surfaces in areas exposed to strong wave surge.

--- SENTENCE 1051 ---
In these areas, *Echinostrephus aciculatus* was sampled with a 25 cm x 25 cm quadrat (= 0.0625 m2).

--- SENTENCE 1052 ---
The quadrat was thrown randomly twice at 5-m intervals and within 1 m of the transect line, yielding 12 samples from which population densities were estimated.

--- SENTENCE 1053 ---
When time permitted, the reef adjacent to transects were examined to record observations of macroinvertebrates not encountered along the transect line.

--- SENTENCE 1054 ---
These records were compiled to compose a faunal list for the areas surveyed.

--- SENTENCE 1055 ---
#### RESULTS

The densities of nonscleractinian macroinvertebrates along 21 belt transects at six sites in Fagatele Bay are presented in Tables 9-12.

--- SENTENCE 1056 ---
Coral reef echinoderms were the predominant macroinvertebrates, occurring on all transect in Fagatele Bay.

--- SENTENCE 1057 ---
At outer sites, where exposure to water motion is greatest (Transect 1 and 6), the small echinoid *Echinostrephus aciculatus* was the principal species.

--- SENTENCE 1058 ---
This species reached densities as great as 208 urchins/m2 on oblique surfaces, where it bores a round burrow into the limestone substrate.

--- SENTENCE 1059 ---
However, in more sheltered areas and on the deeper isobaths, the densities of *Echinostrephus aciculatus* diminished to fewer than 1 individual/m2.

--- SENTENCE 1060 ---
This species occurred on 18 of the 21 transects in Fagatele Bay.

--- SENTENCE 1061 ---
Another echinoid, *Echinometra mathaei,* also occurred with high frequency on transects in Fagatele Bay, but at lower densities than those attained by *Echinostrephus aciculatus.

--- SENTENCE 1062 ---
Echinometra mathaei* occurred on 15 of the 21 transects, and reached densities as great as 0.5 urchins/m2 in the more sheltered areas of Transects 3 and 4.

--- SENTENCE 1063 ---
Although *Echinometra mathaei* occurred with greater frequency on transects in the more sheltered areas, sponges were predominant in terms of biomass present.

--- SENTENCE 1064 ---
The *Dysidea herbacea* species-complex was particularly noteworthy.

--- SENTENCE 1065 ---
Mats of these encrusting sponges attained densities as great as 4.4/m2 at Transect 3.

--- SENTENCE 1066 ---
Considerable areas of substrate were covered by these sponges.

--- SENTENCE 1067 ---
Alcyonacean corals were a conspicuous and abundant component of the macroinvertebrate assemblage in Fagatele Bay.

--- SENTENCE 1068 ---
*C. pachyclados,* a small soft coral, occurred with the highest frequency of all the macroinvertebrates.

--- SENTENCE 1069 ---
pachyclados* was encountered on 19 of 21 transects in Fagatele Bay.

--- SENTENCE 1070 ---
This species occurred at densities in excess of 5 colonies/m2.

--- SENTENCE 1071 ---
Larger species, such as *Sinularia* and *Lobophytum* species, were present but in relatively low densities.

--- SENTENCE 1072 ---
Encrusting mats of the zoanthid *Palythoa tuberculosa* were widespread in Fagatele Bay, but the species reached its greatest density at Transect 5 along the 10-ft and 15-ft isobaths.

--- SENTENCE 1073 ---
Molluscs comprised the most diverse macroinvertebrate assemblage on transects in Fagatele Bay.

--- SENTENCE 1074 ---
The higher diversity was represented by predatory neogastropods, which constituted some 74% of the gastropod assemblage and 40% of total macroinvertebrate diversity in Fagatele Bay.

--- SENTENCE 1075 ---
Mollusc species of interest because of their potential for harvest for human consumption included two species of giant clams and one species of octopus.

--- SENTENCE 1076 ---
*Tridacna maxima* and *Tridacna squamosa* were present on transects, but they occurred as scattered individuals.

--- SENTENCE 1077 ---
One *Octopus cyaneus* was also noted.

--- SENTENCE 1078 ---
Densities of macroinvertebrates occurring on belt transects along the 20-ft isobath at 10 additional sites around Tutuila are presented in Tables 13 and 14.

--- SENTENCE 1079 ---
A species list of macroinvertebrates observed in Fagatele Bay and at 10 additional sites is given in Table 15.

#### DISCUSSION

Several noteworthy changes have occurred in the macroinvertebrate assemblage of Fagatele Bay since the baseline survey in 1985.

--- SENTENCE 1080 ---
The densities of the burrowing echinoid *Echinometra mathaei* have declined significantly on all but two transects (Transect 4, 10-ft and 30-ft isobaths).

--- SENTENCE 1081 ---
High densities of these sea urchins in 1985 (Birkeland et al., 1987) were cause for some concern because of the potential impact on the reef structure of their burrowing.

--- SENTENCE 1082 ---
Formation of urchin grooves, which may be 1 m in length, is a form of bioerosion that could weaken the reef framework and cause slumping.

--- SENTENCE 1083 ---
The drop in *Echinometra mathaei* densities follows the pattern previously reported by Birkeland (1981) in which abundance of herbivorous sea urchins increased following an infestation of *Acanthaster planci* in Palau before World War II and declined over the next decades.

--- SENTENCE 1084 ---
I view the reduction in population densities of *Echinometra mathaei* as a healthy sign for continued recovery of the Fagatele Bay coral reef ecosystem.

--- SENTENCE 1085 ---
The densities of the boring urchin *Echinostrephus aciculatus* remain essentially unchanged since 1985.

--- SENTENCE 1086 ---
However, because the burrow of *Echinstephus aciculatus* is a short (5-6 cm) straight, round hole perpendicular to the substrate, it is less damaging to the reef framework.

--- SENTENCE 1087 ---
Furthermore, this species attains its greatest abundance in areas exposed to considerable wave energy, where the substrate is continually scoured by unconsolidated debris.

--- SENTENCE 1088 ---
Therefore, bioerosion by this species is not a cause for great concern.

--- SENTENCE 1089 ---
There are also indications of increasing diversity within the macroinvertebrate community.

--- SENTENCE 1090 ---
For example, the number of neogastropod species increased from 14 in 1985 to 20 in 1995, while proportionately neogastropods declined from 78% to 74% of the total gastropod assemblage.

--- SENTENCE 1091 ---
Similarly, the diversity of alcyonacean corals increased from unidentified species in two genera in 1985 to five species in three genera in 1995.

--- SENTENCE 1092 ---
Further evidence of the diversity of macroinvertebrates is presented in Table 15.

--- SENTENCE 1093 ---
Of 96 species of macroinvertebrates observed on transects or the reef adjacent to transects at Fagatele Bay and the ten additional sites examined during this study, 81 species (>84%) were observed in Fagatele Bay.

--- SENTENCE 1094 ---
Twenty four species observed at Fagatele Bay were not recorded from the ten other sites.

--- SENTENCE 1095 ---
Significantly, two specimens of the coral reef holothurian *Actinopyga mauritiana* were observed at Fagatele Bay during the 1995 reassessment study.

--- SENTENCE 1096 ---
Although none were observed in 1985, the suitability of habitat for this species in Fagatele Bay was noted (Birkeland et al., 1987).

--- SENTENCE 1097 ---
Also, no *Acanthaster planci* were recorded in 1995, while two were reported in 1985.

--- SENTENCE 1098 ---
One group of macroinvertebrates has increased dramatically in abundance since the baseline survey in 1985.

--- SENTENCE 1099 ---
The encrusting sponge *Dysidea* sp. (*Dysidea herbacea* complex) was not recorded in 1985, but this sponge covered considerable areas of substrate in 1995.

--- SENTENCE 1100 ---
The decline in sea urchins and the increase in sponges and overall diversity may be successional stages in recovery of the ecosystem.

--- SENTENCE 1101 ---
However, as noted by Birkeland et al. (1987), successional patterns within macroinvertebrate communities following devastation of coral reefs by *Acanthaster planci*

have received little attention.

--- SENTENCE 1102 ---
Further monitoring in Fagatele Bay and other areas affected by *Acanthaster planci* will be required to make such a determination

### ALGAL COMMUNITIES

#### by Suzanne Wilkins

#### INTRODUCTION

--- SENTENCE 1471 ---
Most of the older information on algal communities of American Samoa is sparse and frequently quite general (Setchell, 1924; Dahl, 1971; Guinther and Madden, 1980).

--- SENTENCE 1472 ---
A more recent quantitative study of the benthic marine plant community was done by this author in March of 1985 (Birkeland *et al.,* 1987).

--- SENTENCE 1473 ---
The current study was conducted in July of 1995 and followed the same methods and surveyed the same transects in Fagatele Bay and 10 other sites around Tutuila as those of the 1985 survey, with the exception of two sites.

--- SENTENCE 1474 ---
Many algal species are seasonal and since both of the recent surveys have been conducted during different seasons it is difficult to make meaningful comparisons about the algal community and residual or secondary-impact from environmental changes until more data is available.

#### METHODS

Marine plants and the substrate were quantified by the point-quadrat method along 30-m transects following a series of depth contours (10, 15, 30, and 40 feet).

--- SENTENCE 1475 ---
Since areas covered by algal turf, crustose coralline algae, and other encrusting organisms are difficult to measure by dimensions, it is more appropriate to survey such organisms by the point-quadrat method.

--- SENTENCE 1476 ---
This method provides data from which a rapid assessment of percent cover and frequency of occurrence of any algal species is possible.

--- SENTENCE 1477 ---
Thus, distribution patterns, evenness or patchiness of the benthic algal species, can easily be recognized.

--- SENTENCE 1478 ---
This method consists of quantifying organisms or substrate types under the points of intersection of strings tied across a quadrat (25 x 25 cm) frame creating 25 squares with 16 crosspoints.

--- SENTENCE 1479 ---
Whatever algal species occurred under each point was recorded.

--- SENTENCE 1480 ---
In cases when the point fell on two layers of algae the base alga was recorded and the overlying alga was noted as present.

--- SENTENCE 1481 ---
When identification of an alga was impossible to accomplish in the field a specimen was collected and pressed for later identification.

--- SENTENCE 1482 ---
If no alga was found under the point, than whatever was present, e.g., sand, live coral or rubble, was recorded.

--- SENTENCE 1483 ---
The quadrat was tossed randomly at 5-m intervals along the length of the transect.

--- SENTENCE 1484 ---
Therefore, data were collected from 6 quadrats, or at 96 points, along a transect.

--- SENTENCE 1485 ---
Each of these transects were positioned perpendicular to Permanent Transects (1, 2, 3, 4, 5, and 6) established during a previous survey (Birkeland *et al.,* 1987) and followed a series of depth contours (10, 15, 30, or 40 feet; 3.0, 4.6, 9.1, or 12.2 m).

--- SENTENCE 1486 ---
Permanent Transect 1 accommodated transects at 15, 30, and 40 feet, Transect 6 had only transects at 20 and 40 feet, while all other Permanent Transects had transects at all four depths.

--- SENTENCE 1487 ---
Percent cover for each transect was calculated by taking the number of points occupied by a particular categories divided by the total number of points per transect.

--- SENTENCE 1488 ---
Frequency of occurrence was calculated by taking the number if quadrat tosses in which a benthic constituent occurred,

divided by the number of tosses per transect.

--- SENTENCE 1489 ---
Both cover and frequency values were converted to percent by multiplying by 100.

--- SENTENCE 1490 ---
Other algal species seen along the transect were recorded as observed.

--- SENTENCE 1491 ---
In addition, 10 previously established 30-m transects at a depth of 20 (6.1 m) feet were resurveyed around Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 1492 ---
The same methods were applied as described above.

#### RESULTS AND DISCUSSION

Results of the quantitative survey of marine benthic algae in Fagatele Bay are presented in Tables 16 and 17.

--- SENTENCE 1493 ---
A total of 26 species of benthic marine algae were encountered along the 21 transects in Fagatele Bay.

--- SENTENCE 1494 ---
Overall percent cover of the marine benthic algal community was 75 (s=12) of which crustose and articulate coralline algae made up 69 (s=14) percent.

--- SENTENCE 1495 ---
*Peysonnelia* (maximum cover 25%), a very common component, formed a firmly adherent reddish crust covering dead corals and rubble over large areas especially along the 30 and 40 feet depth contour.

--- SENTENCE 1496 ---
*Porolithon onkodes* (as high as 80 percent cover), the most conspicuous component of the algal cover throughout Fagatele Bay, appears to have formed an almost continuous smooth crust covering, cementing and stabilizing the loose surface below.

--- SENTENCE 1497 ---
The well scrubbed appearance of the *Porolithon* crust showed relatively little evidence of epiphytic algal growth, only *Gelidium pussilum, Ceramium* and *Herposiphonia* were found tightly adhered to the surface of this crust.

--- SENTENCE 1498 ---
*Amphiroa* and *Chilosporum*, and other common component of the coralline algal assemblage, were also frequently found entangled with other species of algae forming dense clumps.

--- SENTENCE 1499 ---
The algal composition of these clumps was similar to that of algal turfs and was composed of algal species such as *Dictyota, Dictyopteris, Ceramium, Polysiphonia, Jania, Herposiphonia, Gelidium, Gelidiopsis, Gelidiella, Sphacelaria,* and *Pterocladia.

--- SENTENCE 1500 ---
*

Tables 19 and 20 provide a overview of the benthic marine floral community of the two recent surveys (Birkeland et al., 1987 and the 1995 survey).

--- SENTENCE 1501 ---
They summarize the overall percent algal and coralline algal cover along the four depth contours (Table 19) and the Permanent Transects in Fagatele Bay (Table 20).

--- SENTENCE 1502 ---
Encrusting and articulate coralline algal cover generally increased while percent cover of non-coralline algae decreased since the last survey.

--- SENTENCE 1503 ---
The composition of the plant assemblages from the 10 transects (Table 18) surveyed at sites around Tutuila were similar to those found in Fagatele.

--- SENTENCE 1504 ---
The total number of plant species recorded along the transects is lower than that from the previous survey (53 species in 1985 and 27 species in the 1995 survey), however, the mean value for percent algal cover increased slightly since 1985 (from 56.1 ± 24 to 62.0 ± 24).

--- SENTENCE 1505 ---
Previously recorded fleshy and filamentous algae seem to be confined to algal turf assemblages or were hidden in depressions and interstices of the substrate, thus many areas seem to have a much "cleaner" appearance.

--- SENTENCE 1506 ---
Coralline algae, in particular encrusting coralline algae, showed an overall increase in percent cover (from 38.4 ± 24 to 48.1 ± 19) from the values recorded during the 1985 survey (Table 21).

--- SENTENCE 1507 ---
Frequency and percent cover of the benthic flora in Fagatele Bay, American Samoa, (transects 1, 2 3).

--- SENTENCE 1508 ---
Plain numbers indicate percent cover, numbers in parenthesis indicate frequency of occurrence converted to percent (see Methods in text).

--- SENTENCE 1509 ---
Algal species occurring epiphytically on other algae or occurring in the vicinity of the transect are marked with and X.**

### FISH COMMUNITIES

#### by Alison Green

#### INTRODUCTION

--- SENTENCE 1800 ---
The coral reefs of American Samoa have suffered many destructive impacts in the last two decades, including a major outbreak of the corallivorous starfish *

--- SENTENCE 1801 ---
Acanthaster planci,* two devastating hurricanes and a mass coral bleaching event (see Coral Communities).

--- SENTENCE 1802 ---
In addition, the reefs in some locations, such as Pago Pago Harbor, have been subjected to human impacts including sedimentation, eutrophication and pollution (Craig *et al.* 1995, see Coral Communities).

--- SENTENCE 1803 ---
As a direct result of these disturbances, the coral reefs of Fagatele Bay National Marine Sanctuary and other locations around Tutuila Island have changed dramatically in recent years.

--- SENTENCE 1804 ---
Of particular importance have been the physical changes to the reef framework and the decrease in coral cover that have occurred in some locations (see Coral Communities).

--- SENTENCE 1805 ---
Coral communities provide important habitat for fishes that inhabit coral reefs and a decline in their condition may have major consequences for the associated fish fauna.

--- SENTENCE 1806 ---
The purpose of this study was to determine if the coral reef fish communities in Fagatele Bay and elsewhere around Tutuila Island have changed substantially over the last two decades along with the changes in their habitat characteristics.

--- SENTENCE 1807 ---
Changes in the fish communities are described based on the results of several fish surveys that have been done over the last 18 years (Birkeland *et al.* 1987, Birkeland *et al.* 1994, this study).

#### METHODS

This study is divided into three sections which vary in terms of the sites surveyed,

--- SENTENCE 1808 ---
the methods used and the duration of the study:

#### *Changes in the fish communities of Fagatele Bay from 1985 to 1995*

The changes in the fish communities in Fagatele Bay are described based on the results of three surveys over the last ten years: 1985 (Birkeland *et al.* 1987), 1988 (Birkeland *et al.* 1994) and 1995 (this study).

--- SENTENCE 1809 ---
The physical and biological characteristics of the Bay are described in Birkeland *et al.*

--- SENTENCE 1810 ---
(1987), as well as a description of each transect including its exact location (see Fig.

--- SENTENCE 1811 ---
2 in Coral Communities).

--- SENTENCE 1812 ---
All transects included in previous surveys were re-surveyed in this study (Table 31).

--- SENTENCE 1813 ---
This included counts at two depths (9m and 12m) at Sites 1 and 6, and at four depths (3m, 6m, 9m and 12m) at Sites 2 through 5.

--- SENTENCE 1814 ---
Each transect was established as close as possible to the transect locations used during previous surveys of the Bay.

--- SENTENCE 1815 ---
In addition, fishes were surveyed on the reef flat at two sites (Sites b and c: see Fig. 2 in Coral Communities) for the first time in this survey (Table 27).

--- SENTENCE 1816 ---
Standard transect dimensions of 30 x 2 m were used throughout this study, and surveys were done using the fish survey techniques described below.

--- SENTENCE 1817 ---
Fish communities are compared through time based on their species richness and abundance.

--- SENTENCE 1818 ---
#### *Short term changes in the fish communities around Tutuila Island (from 1988 to 1995)

--- SENTENCE 1819 ---
*

The short term changes in the fish communities around Tutuila Island were examined based on two surveys done over the last six years: 1988 (Birkeland *et al.* 1994) and 1995 (this study).

--- SENTENCE 1820 ---
In each survey, fishes were counted at two depths (3m and 6m) at ten sites around Tutuila (Sites 1- 4 and 7-12 in Fig.

--- SENTENCE 1821 ---
1 in Coral Communities).

--- SENTENCE 1822 ---
As described by Randall and Birkeland (see Coral Communities), these sites were originally established in pairs of exposed and sheltered sites around the island.

--- SENTENCE 1823 ---
Transects were surveyed using the same dimensions as those used in Fagatele Bay (30 x 2 m).

--- SENTENCE 1824 ---
Fish communities are compared among years on the basis of their species richness and abundance.

#### *Long term changes in the fish communities around Tutuila Island (from 1977 to 1995)*

This long-term study was originally designed to describe the impact of an outbreak of *Acanthaster planci* on the reef fish communities on Tutuila Island.

--- SENTENCE 1825 ---
One transect was surveyed at each of three sites (Fagatele Bay, Sita Bay and Cape Larsen: see Fig. 1 in Coral Communities) on four occasions over an 18 year period.

--- SENTENCE 1826 ---
These surveys included times before and after the *Acanthaster* outbreak that occurred in late 1978: 1977-1978 and 1985 (Birkeland *et al.* 1987), 1988 (Birkeland *et al.* 1994) and 1995 (this study).

--- SENTENCE 1827 ---
Two of these sites (Fagatele Bay and Cape Larsen) were severely impacted at the time of the outbreak, while the other site (Sita Bay) was largely untouched (Birkeland *et al.* 1987).

--- SENTENCE 1828 ---
Since that time all three sites have been subjected to two major hurricanes (in 1990 and 1991) and a mass coral bleaching event (in 1994).

--- SENTENCE 1829 ---
Larger transects (100 x 2 m) were used here than in the other two sections, because these transects were originally designed as part of a different project (see Wass 1982).

--- SENTENCE 1830 ---
The exact location of each transect is described in Birkeland *et al.*

--- SENTENCE 1831 ---
care was taken to re-establish the transects as close as possible to their original locations.

--- SENTENCE 1832 ---
Changes in the fish community at each of these sites was described by comparing trends in species richness and abundance over the last 18 years.

--- SENTENCE 1833 ---
These changes were then examined in more detail by comparing the number of individuals of each family that were recorded in each survey.

--- SENTENCE 1834 ---
Changes in the fish assemblages were also examined at the species level by comparing the abundance of each species of the two most abundant families, the Pomacentridae and Acanthuridae, during each survey.

#### *Fish survey techniques*

Fishes were surveyed using the same underwater visual census techniques that were used in previous surveys, which are described in detail in Birkeland *et al.*

--- SENTENCE 1835 ---
In each year of the survey, a single observer did the fish counts.

--- SENTENCE 1836 ---
However, the surveys were done by different observers in different years: R. Wass in 1977-1978 and 1985 (Birkeland *et al.* 1987), S. Amesbury in 1988 (Birkeland *et al.* 1994) and A. Green in 1995 (this study).

--- SENTENCE 1837 ---
#### RESULTS

A total of 6792 individuals of 191 species were recorded on all of the 45 transects re-surveyed in Fagatele Bay and elsewhere around Tutuila Island in 1995 (see Tables 22-25).

--- SENTENCE 1838 ---
An additional 30 species were observed off the transects, one of which *(Acanthurus albipectoralis)* was a new record for American Samoa.

### *Changes in the fish communities of Fagatele Bay from 1985 to 1995*

A total of 133 fish species were recorded on the reef slope transects in Fagatele Bay in 1995.

--- SENTENCE 1839 ---
This fish community was characterized by three acanthurid species *(Ctenochaetus striatus, Acanthurus nigrofuscus* and *Acanthurus nigricans),* one labrid species *(Thalassoma quinquevittatum),* five pomacentrid species *(Chrysiptera leucompoma, Pomacentrus vaiuli, Chromis acares, Pomacentrus brachialis* and *Plectrogylphidodon lacrymatus)* and one species of scarid *(Scarus sordidus)*

--- SENTENCE 1840 ---
Tables 22a-c.

Species richness was slightly higher on the reef slope in 1995 than it was in 1985 (Table 26).

--- SENTENCE 1841 ---
This was despite the fact that the area surveyed in 1985 was only 60% of that surveyed in 1995 (Table 26).

--- SENTENCE 1842 ---
In contrast, species richness in both 1985 and 1995 was almost twice that recorded in 1988 (Table 26).

--- SENTENCE 1843 ---
These trends in species richness were consistent at all depths surveyed in the Bay (Fig. 3).

--- SENTENCE 1844 ---
A total of 2501 individuals were recorded on the reef slope transects at Fagatele Bay in 1995.

--- SENTENCE 1845 ---
Fish abundance was slightly less than that recorded in 1985 (Table 26), although the area surveyed in 1995 was almost twice as large as that surveyed in 1985 (Table 26).

--- SENTENCE 1846 ---
In a similar pattern to that recorded for species richness, the number of individuals recorded in 1988 was almost half that recorded in 1995 (Table 26).

--- SENTENCE 1847 ---
This trend in abundance was also consistent across depths within the Bay (Fig. 3).

--- SENTENCE 1848 ---
Fishes were surveyed on the reef flats in Fagatele Bay for the first time in 1995, which added another nine species and 449 individuals to the total count for the Bay that year.

--- SENTENCE 1849 ---
The reef flat fish community was characterized by three pomacentrid species *(Stegastes albifasciatus, Chrysiptera cyanea* and *Chrysiptera leucopoma),* three acanthurid species *(Ctenochaetus striatus, Acanthurus nigrofuscus* and *Acanthurus triostegus),* one labrid species *(Thalassoma hardwicke)* and unidentified juvenile scarids (Table 23).

--- SENTENCE 1850 ---
Three of these species were not abundant on the adjacent reef slope *(S. albifasciatus, A. triostegus and T. hardwicke:*

--- SENTENCE 1851 ---
Species richness on the reef flat was slightly less than that recorded on the reef slope in the same year (1995: Fig. 3a).

--- SENTENCE 1852 ---
In contrast, fish abundance on the reef flat was more than twice as high as that recorded on the reef slope in the same year (1995: Fig. 3b).

--- SENTENCE 1853 ---
This was mostly because of the higher abundance of pomacentrids on the reef flat (Table 23) compared to the reef slope (Tables 22a-c) in the Bay.

!

--- SENTENCE 1854 ---
3 Mean (and se) species richness (a) and abundance (b) of fishes in Fagatele Bay during each of three surveys over the last ten years.

--- SENTENCE 1855 ---
Please note that the number of transects surveyed varied among depths and years (see Table 31 ).

#### *Short term changes in the fish communities around Tutuila Island (from 1988 to 1995)*

This short-term study did not detect any clear patterns of variation in the fish communities around Tutuila Island associated with exposure, depth or year (Fig. 4, Tables 24a-c).

--- SENTENCE 1856 ---
Fish communities did not vary between exposed and sheltered sites in any consistent manner.

--- SENTENCE 1857 ---
In some cases, species richness and abundance was lower at sheltered than exposed sites (e.g. Fagasa vs Cape Larsen respectively: Fig. 4).

--- SENTENCE 1858 ---
However this pattern was more the exception than the rule, with species richness and abundance varying in no consistent pattern with exposure.

--- SENTENCE 1859 ---
Fish communities did not vary in any consistent pattern associated with depth either (Fig. 4).

--- SENTENCE 1860 ---
At some sites, species richness or abundance was higher at 6m than at 3m. However, the opposite was true at other sites.

--- SENTENCE 1861 ---
Similarly, fish communities around the island showed no consistent differences between the two years of the survey.

--- SENTENCE 1862 ---
At most sites, species richness was much higher in 1995 compared to 1988 (Fig. 4).

--- SENTENCE 1863 ---
However this was not true at 3m at Fagafue or at 6m in Massacre Bay, where species richness was similar in both years.

--- SENTENCE 1864 ---
At most sites, fish abundance was also higher in 1995 compared to 1988 (Fig. 4b).

--- SENTENCE 1865 ---
However the opposite was true at 6m at Massacre Bay and Rainmaker, where fish abundance was lower in 1995 compared to 1988.

#### *Long term changes in the fish communities around Tutuila Island (from 1977 to 1995)*

Some changes were detected in the fish communities at Fagatele Bay, Sita Bay and Cape Larsen over the last 18 years (Fig. 5).

--- SENTENCE 1866 ---
Species richness was similar at each site in 1977-1978, 1985 and 1995, but was much lower in 1988 (Fig. 5a).

--- SENTENCE 1867 ---
In contrast, fish abundance was higher in the two earlier surveys (1985 and 1977-1978), and lower in the two later surveys (1995 and 1988:

--- SENTENCE 1868 ---
These trends in species richness and abundance through time were consistent at all three sites (Fig. 5).

--- SENTENCE 1869 ---
Changes were also evident over the last two decades in the composition of the fish communities at the three sites.

--- SENTENCE 1870 ---
Comparisons among families showed that the most dramatic change has been in the abundance of the dominant family, the Pomacentridae (Fig. 6).

--- SENTENCE 1871 ---
In the last survey in 1995, there were only 30-50% as many pomacentrids as there were in first survey in 1977-1978.

--- SENTENCE 1872 ---
An examination of this family at the species level showed that the most obvious change has been the dramatic decline in the abundance of *Plectroglyphidodon dickii* throughout the study (Fig. 7).

--- SENTENCE 1873 ---
*P. dickii* was the most abundant pomacentrid species in 1977-1978, but it is now relatively uncommon.

--- SENTENCE 1874 ---
In fact, there were only 1-9% as many individuals of this species recorded in 1995 as there were in 1977-1978.

--- SENTENCE 1875 ---
At Fagatele Bay and Cape Larsen, this decrease in abundance occurred sometime between 1977-1978 and 1985.

--- SENTENCE 1876 ---
However at Sita Bay, this decline did not occur until after 1985.

--- SENTENCE 1877 ---
Some other species, such as *Pomacentrus brachialis* and *Chromis acares,* have also decreased in abundance at some sites over time.

--- SENTENCE 1878 ---
In contrast, one species, *Chrysiptera cyanea,* has increased in abundance at one of the study sites (Cape Larsen: Fig. 7).

--- SENTENCE 1879 ---
In contrast to the pomacentrids, the Family Acanthuridae has been relatively stable throughout the study (Fig.

--- SENTENCE 1880 ---
The obvious exception to this was in 1985 when there was a peak in acanthurid abundance that occurred at all three sites (Fig.

--- SENTENCE 1881 ---
However Birkeland *et al.* (1987)

reported that the vast majority of these individuals were juvenile *Ctenochaetus striatus* which had recently recruited to the reef (see Fig. 8 also).

--- SENTENCE 1882 ---
Observations at that time suggested that many of the individuals were in bad condition and were unlikely to survive to recruit to the adult population (Birkeland *et al.* 1987).

--- SENTENCE 1883 ---
In fact, this does appear to have been the case, since this increase in abundance was not maintained in subsequent years (Fig.

--- SENTENCE 1884 ---
With the exception of this *C. striatus* peak in 1985, there were no apparent changes in the relative abundance of any acanthurid species through time (Fig.

--- SENTENCE 1885 ---
The other families represented in this study showed no obvious trends in abundance over time that were consistent at all three sites (Fig. 9).

--- SENTENCE 1886 ---
Only one family, the Scaridae, showed a dramatic increase in abundance, although this was apparent at one site only (Fagatele Bay: Fig.

--- SENTENCE 1887 ---
9, Table 26).

#### DISCUSSION

--- SENTENCE 1894 ---
The coral reefs of Fagatele Bay and elsewhere around Tutuila Island have suffered many major impacts in the last two decades (see Introduction).

--- SENTENCE 1895 ---
These impacts have resulted in physical and biological changes to the coral communities, which provide important habitat for their associated fish fauna (see Introduction).

--- SENTENCE 1896 ---
This study has demonstrated that the fish communities in Fagatele Bay and elsewhere around Tutuila Island have also changed over the last two decades, concomitant with these changes in their habitat characteristics.

--- SENTENCE 1897 ---
However the degree to which these assemblages appear to have changed depends on the index used to assess the changes.

--- SENTENCE 1898 ---
Species richness does not appear to have decreased throughout the course of this study, with similar species richness recorded during the surveys in 1977-1978, 1985 and 1994 (Figs. 3, 5).

--- SENTENCE 1899 ---
In contrast, species richness was consistently much lower in the 1988 survey than in those done in the other years (Figs. 3, 5).

--- SENTENCE 1900 ---
However this was probably the result of a difference in methodology between surveys and not an actual decline in species richness that year.

--- SENTENCE 1901 ---
The 1988 survey was done while other groups of divers were present in the water at the same time (C. Birkeland pers.

--- SENTENCE 1902 ---
comm.), which was not the case in the other surveys.

--- SENTENCE 1903 ---
As a result, the presence of other divers may have caused a greater disturbance to the fish community in 1988, resulting in a lower number of species being observed that year.

--- SENTENCE 1904 ---
This suggestion is supported by my observations that many fishes either hid or swam rapidly away when other divers entered the survey area (A. Green pers.

--- SENTENCE 1905 ---
Therefore, as a result of this difference in methodology, the 1988 survey was probably not comparable to surveys done in the other years.

--- SENTENCE 1906 ---
In contrast to species richness, fish abundance appears to have decreased over time, with the highest number of individuals recorded in the earliest two surveys and the lowest recorded in the last two surveys (Figs. 3, 5).

--- SENTENCE 1907 ---
Once again, the lowest abundances were consistently recorded in 1988 (Figs. 3, 5).

--- SENTENCE 1908 ---
This was probably due to the same methodological differences that resulted in the lower species richness detected in that year (see above).

--- SENTENCE 1909 ---
The difference between the survey methods used in 1988 and 1995 may also be partly responsible for the fact that the short term study of the ten sites around the island (from 1988 to 1995), failed to produce any consistent patterns of species richness or abundance with exposure, depth or year.

--- SENTENCE 1910 ---
For this reason I recommend that this section of the study be disregarded until surveys can be done in future years using the same methods as those used in 1995.

--- SENTENCE 1911 ---
The most dramatic changes in the fish communities around Tutuila Island have been in the changes in abundances of some families and species over the last two decades.

--- SENTENCE 1912 ---
The most abundant family, the Pomacentridae, is now represented by only 30-50% as many individuals as it was in the 1970s.

--- SENTENCE 1913 ---
This decline was largely due to a 91-99% decrease in the abundance of one species, *Plectroglyphidodon dickii,* which may be explained in the context of habitat degradation.

--- SENTENCE 1914 ---
*P. dickii* is a territorial inhabitant of robustly branching *Pocillopora* and *Acropora* corals (Myers 1989), and since coral cover has decreased dramatically at these study sites throughout the study, it is likely that habitat degradation has been responsible for the decline in this species.

--- SENTENCE 1915 ---
This suggestion is further supported by the fact that the decline at Fagatele Bay and Cape Larsen occurred after the 1977-1978 survey, which coincided with the decrease in coral cover at those sites due to an outbreak of the crown-of-thorns starfish (Birkeland *et al.* 1994).

--- SENTENCE 1916 ---
In contrast, the

decrease in the abundance of *P. dickii* did not occur at Sita Bay at that time, since that site was unaffected by the starfish outbreak (Birkeland *et al.* 1994).

--- SENTENCE 1917 ---
In contrast, the decline of this species occurred sometime between 1985 and 1994 at Sita Bay, during which time two major hurricanes caused extensive damage to the coral communities around the island (see Coral Communities).

--- SENTENCE 1918 ---
The decline in other pomacentrid species, *Chromis acares* and *Pomacentrus brachialis,* may also be related to the decrease in live coral cover at these sites.

--- SENTENCE 1919 ---
In contrast, the one pomacentrid species that increased in abundance, *Chrysiptera cyanea,* is known to be associated with rubble patches that may have increased throughout the course of the study.

--- SENTENCE 1920 ---
However the case for habitat degradation being responsible for these changes in abundance is largely circumstantial, and it is possible that other confounding factors such as recruitment variability may have contributed to these patterns also.

--- SENTENCE 1921 ---
In contrast to the pomacentrids, the Family Acanthuridae did not show a similar decline in abundance throughout the study.

--- SENTENCE 1922 ---
*Ctenochaetus striatus* has remained the dominant acanthurid species on the reef slopes of Fagatele Bay and elsewhere around the island throughout the last two decades.

--- SENTENCE 1923 ---
Furthermore, the relative abundance of the other acanthurid species remained similar throughout the study.

--- SENTENCE 1924 ---
The fact that this family did not appear to decrease in response to the changes in the coral communities was not surprising, since they are roving herbivores that are less likely to be affected by the loss of coral cover than are small site-attached pomacentrids.

--- SENTENCE 1925 ---
In fact the only family that showed an increase in abundance was another family of roving herbivores, the Scaridae (Fig. 9).

--- SENTENCE 1926 ---
However, this increase was apparent at one site only (Fagatele Bay).

--- SENTENCE 1927 ---
At this point, it is important to take note of the limitations of this study.

--- SENTENCE 1928 ---
One important limitation, the problem of different methodologies among years, has already been mentioned.

--- SENTENCE 1929 ---
However, inter-observer bias may have been a confounding factor in this study in more ways than one.

--- SENTENCE 1930 ---
For example, the observers may have differed in their ability to judge the width of the transect, or their ability to identify species.

--- SENTENCE 1931 ---
Another methodological problem was the fact that the surveys were done at different months in different years (the 1995 survey was done in July, while the others were done in April).

--- SENTENCE 1932 ---
Consequently, seasonal differences could have contributed to some of the patterns described in this study.

--- SENTENCE 1933 ---
Since methodological differences among surveys can cause substantial problems in the long term monitoring of fish communities, it is recommended that the same observer using a standardized set of methods be used in all subsequent surveys.

--- SENTENCE 1934 ---
Another limitation of this survey was the lack of replication in some aspects of the study design.

--- SENTENCE 1935 ---
In other situations, unequal replication of transects was also limiting, because it made testing for significant differences among years and sites problematic.

--- SENTENCE 1936 ---
In future years it is recommended that the experimental design for this study be expanded to rectify this situation.

--- SENTENCE 1937 ---
Despite these limitations, the results of this study are still valuable, since they are quantitative and comprise a long time series of information.

--- SENTENCE 1938 ---
In conclusion, the fish communities of Fagatele Bay and elsewhere around Tutuila Island have changed in the last two decades.

--- SENTENCE 1939 ---
This is probably the result of habitat degradation caused by the effects of several major disturbances on the coral reefs.

--- SENTENCE 1940 ---
At present, these reefs are in an important stage of recovery.

--- SENTENCE 1941 ---
Fortunately, coral colonies are recruiting and growing quickly at most sites

around the island (see Coral Communities, A. Green pers.

--- SENTENCE 1942 ---
obs.) and recovery appears to be well underway (see Coral Communities).

--- SENTENCE 1943 ---
In the absence of any major perturbations in the next few years, most of the coral communities in this study should continue to recover (see Coral Communities) and, along with them, their associated fish communities.

## APPENDIX A: Coralline Lethal Orange Disease

#### by Charles Birkeland

The recently discovered "orange band" disease was observed at several locations in Fagatele Bay during the 1995 assessment period.

--- SENTENCE 2723 ---
The host of the disease is encrusted coralline algae and the cause or pathogen is at present not known.

--- SENTENCE 2724 ---
In the field the disease is easily recognized by the presence of a circular, narrow, bright orange ring that occurs on encrusted coralline algal surfaces (Fig. 10).

--- SENTENCE 2725 ---
Apparently the disease starts at some location, possibly at a damaged or stressed point, and then expands in a centrifugal manner leaving a trailing white band, about as wide as the orange band itself.

--- SENTENCE 2726 ---
The white band or area is presumably the crustose algal region that has been killed of affected.

!

--- SENTENCE 2727 ---
Coralline lethal orange disease

Commonly the orange band and associated white zone appear as incomplete circles, forming semicircles or crescent shapes.

--- SENTENCE 2728 ---
Close inspection of the orange and white bands and the enclosed circular area reveals that the crustose algae recovers or rapidly recolonizes the affected regions.

--- SENTENCE 2729 ---
In no instances was the enclosed area of the peripheral bands completely killed (white).

--- SENTENCE 2730 ---
Generally the enclosed area was an overall pink or purplish red or a somewhat mottled mixture of these colors.

--- SENTENCE 2731 ---
In crescent-shaped affected areas it was sometimes difficult to discern the area behind the orange and white bands from the normal surrounding algal encrusted surfaces.

--- SENTENCE 2732 ---
Observations of several orange band occurrences at transect 4 during the two-week duration of our assessment period revealed no apparent expansion or contraction of the orange band.

--- SENTENCE 2733 ---
Possibly the rate of growth is slow, sporadic, or was dormant.

--- SENTENCE 2734 ---
In addition to general qualitative observations within the bay, the frequency of occurrence was determined at all six transects on the reef platform and the 3 and 5 meter depth locations during the same time the plotless point-quarter coral transects were run.

--- SENTENCE 2735 ---
At each transect point a search radius of one meter was used in each quadrant.

--- SENTENCE 2736 ---
It was noticed that a common grey colored encrusting sponge (*Dysidea*) was also conspicuously growing over a number of substrates, including crustose algae.

--- SENTENCE 2737 ---
The frequency of the grey sponge was also recorded along with the occurrence of the orange band disease (Table A-1, below).

--- SENTENCE 2738 ---
The frequency of the orange band disease was expected to be higher from just general observations, but this was apparently an illusion because of its conspicuousness.

--- SENTENCE 2739 ---
A separate 50 meter long transect was laid down at a rubble covered reentry area at 3 meters depth at transect 4 because of the apparent abundance of the orange band occurrences there, but none was encountered with a 1 meter search radius.

--- SENTENCE 2740 ---
General observations, as well as the transect data, showed a greater abundance of the orange band disease at transect 5.

--- SENTENCE 2741 ---
No occurrences of the orange band disease was recorded from any of our other 12 monitoring stations around Tutuila, but one occurrence was observed at the airport dredge site.

--- SENTENCE 2742 ---
In conclusion, it appears that the orange band disease is not a serious threat to the health of the reefs of Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 2743 ---
The disease has a very low frequency of occurrence, and apparently the infected areas quickly recover and become reestablished by crustose coralline algae.

--- SENTENCE 2744 ---
The common, but not so conspicuous, grey sponge was affecting about four times as much reef area—that included coral as well as coralline encrusted areas—as the orange band disease in Fagatele Bay.

--- SENTENCE 2745 ---
The frequency of occurrence for the orange band disease and grey sponge per quadrant for Transects 1-6.

|        | Quadrant<br>/depth | CLOD | Dysidea | number<br>of quadrants |
|--------|--------------------|------|---------|------------------------|

### ACKNOWLEDGEMENTS

Our project with the Fagatele Bay National Marine Sanctuary (12-28 July 1995) was a success largely because of logistic help of the Department of Marine & Wildlife Resources (DMWR), Government of American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 2751 ---
We were all most impressed with the cooperation among programs under the administration of Raymond Tulafono.

--- SENTENCE 2752 ---
The Department of Marine and Wildlife Resources provided boat, fuel, scuba tanks, air fills and, most importantly, the expert assistance of Fale Tuilagi and Elia Henry.

--- SENTENCE 2753 ---
Fale and Elia are extraordinary workers.

--- SENTENCE 2754 ---
Elia consistently put all his effort into each of our endeavors.

--- SENTENCE 2755 ---
He was always full of energy and completely and competently committed to the tasks at hand.

--- SENTENCE 2756 ---
Fale had a great deal of technical skill.

--- SENTENCE 2757 ---
He organized the logistics of most of our trips, which included such administrative matters as obtaining permission from local residents for access to the Fagatele Bay National Marine Sanctuary via land and permission to launch a boat in the village of Onenoa.

--- SENTENCE 2758 ---
He was able to launch and retrieve the boat from a rocky beach which we would have thought was impossible.

--- SENTENCE 2759 ---
In Fagatele Bay, he was able to oversee the safe and efficient operation of scientists in four different sections of the bay simultaneously.

--- SENTENCE 2760 ---
He was a remarkably effective technician and was present every day, even on Sundays and holidays, to help us take advantage of every day of our limited visit.

--- SENTENCE 2761 ---
Ultimately, of course, the whole project was brought about by Nancy Daschbach, Fagatele Bay National Marine Sanctuary Coordinator.

--- SENTENCE 2762 ---
She organized and secured the logistics and equipment, and she participated in most of the field work, even driving us to access by land and guiding us down the path by land when the seas were too rough to go by boat.

--- SENTENCE 2763 ---
Final typesetting and document preparation was performed by Good Work(s), an American Samoa consulting company.

--- SENTENCE 2764 ---
Nancy Daschbach provided the final editing.
